<result># How an idea becomes a frozen `spec.v1.json` in FlightDeck

**A note on repo state first**, because it changes several of your answers: the checked-out branch is `main` (tip `5300f0d6 Add agent definitions to main`). It carries the spec-chain agent definitions, the manuals, the schemas, the templates and the checks — but it does **not** carry the `fc` CLI, the `launch/` runtime tree, or `crew/README.md`/`crew/explorer.md`. Those exist on a sibling branch, `flightcrew-buildout-v1` (and its parent commits `5cad9b9`/`acafec4`), which built the whole orchestration system (including this very spec-chain) as a self-hosted project. I read both: what's live on `main` for the operational description, and the buildout branch for the fixed paths and roster you specifically asked about (`crew/README.md`) and for `design.md`. I flag every place this distinction matters.

---

## 1. The interview mechanism, in operational detail

The interview is not a chat script — it's a small file-based state machine that the spec-builder agent drives, defined in `flightdeck/flightcrew/crew/spec-builder.md` and its companion rules in `library/spec/interview-session-conventions.md`.

**Where it lives.** Everything the builder writes goes under one run folder and nowhere else:

&gt; "All under the run folder, and nowhere else: `spec.v&lt;n&gt;.json`; `interview/problems.json` (the register); `interview/bundles/&lt;marker&gt;.json` (one per problem asked); `interview/findings.json` (the ledger)." — `flightdeck/flightcrew/crew/spec-builder.md:49`

The run folder itself is a session input the invoker must supply: `dev/workspace/runs/&lt;spec-name&gt;/` (`spec-builder.md:18`). On the buildout branch this run folder concept maps onto the canonical spec home `flightdeck/launch/specs/&lt;spec-name&gt;/`, whose `interview/` subdirectory is explicitly "never copied" into a launch (`crew/README.md`, buildout branch, "Fixed paths" section — quoted in full below).

**problems.json — the register.** A "problem" is any hole the builder can name during drafting. It is filed with a *kind*:

&gt; "Every hole you can name goes in the problem register with a kind: gap, dual-path, tradeoff, failure-mode, hard-part, or whatever the hole actually is. A problem cites the explorer return or the sentence of the intention that revealed it." — `spec-builder.md:26`

The kind list is explicitly open-ended ("or whatever the hole actually is" — same line, and confirmed at `library/spec/interview-session-conventions.md:39`). Each problem has a lifecycle: registered → asked (a bundle exists for it) → closed (an answer became a node) or → reopened if the answer itself revealed a new hole (`spec-builder.md:28`, `interview-session-conventions.md:42`).

**bundles/&lt;marker&gt;.json — the question a human actually sees.** One bundle per problem, addressed to the provider:

&gt; "bundle the hard questions: one bundle per problem, at most five questions, each with its explanation, its options, a recommendation where you have grounds, and a comment field." — `spec-builder.md:27`

The companion doc gives the same shape with the file mechanics spelled out:

&gt; "Questions reach the provider in bundles, one bundle per problem, marked with the problem's id. A bundle carries at most five questions, each with an explanation, options where the answer is a choice, an optional recommendation, and a comment field; the bundle itself takes a comment." — `interview-session-conventions.md:49-50`

So a bundle file's shape, reconstructed from these two sources, is: a `marker` (the problem id), up to 5 questions each with `explanation`, `options` (when the answer is a choice), an optional `recommendation`, a per-question `comment` slot, and a bundle-level `comment` slot.

**How it reaches the human — deliberately not specified as one channel.** This is explicit and important:

&gt; "How a bundle is shown to the provider — a page, the question tool, plain chat — is a surface's concern and never yours; the bundle file is the contract." — `spec-builder.md:49`
&gt; "How a bundle is shown — a page, the question tool, plain chat — is a surface's concern; the bundle contract is the seam, and any surface that honours it may serve a session." — `interview-session-conventions.md:53`

In practice, because the spec-builder's frontmatter grants it the `AskUserQuestion` tool (`spec-builder.md:4`), the most direct surface in a Claude Code session **is** `AskUserQuestion` — the builder can render a bundle's questions through it turn by turn. But the file is what's authoritative; a human could equally be handed the bundle JSON to edit directly, or read it off a rendered page. Nothing in the definition requires `AskUserQuestion` specifically.

**How an answer gets back in.** Not through the tool's structured return alone — the contract is a specific chat block format:

&gt; "Answers arrive in chat as a block headed `## &lt;marker&gt;`, then per question `### &lt;id&gt;`, `answer:`, optional `comment:`, then an optional `bundle comment:`." — `spec-builder.md:49`

and:

&gt; "Answers return under the same marker, with unanswered questions marked as such; several bundles may return in one block." — `interview-session-conventions.md:51`

**findings.json — the ledger.** This is not for interview answers; it's the record of every judge failure and every attacker finding, each resolved to exactly one of three states:

&gt; "Every finding enters the ledger with exactly one state — asked, absorbed, or rejected with a reason." — `spec-builder.md:30`
&gt; "Every judge failure and attacker finding is dispensed by the interviewer through a ledger with exactly one state: asked, absorbed into a named node, or rejected with a reason. A finding may be a false positive; rejecting one with its reason is the interviewer's job, not the provider's." — `interview-session-conventions.md:60-61`

**Session interruption.** If the provider leaves mid-session, nothing is dispatched onward:

&gt; "A provider who leaves mid-session leaves a saved draft with its open questions and register, no judge dispatched, no handoff; the next session resumes from those problems with a wave scoped to their domains, not from the beginning." — `spec-builder.md:37`

I looked for a real, committed example of `problems.json`/`bundles/*.json`/`findings.json` to quote from — none exists in this repository at any commit (interview folders are explicitly excluded from being copied/committed per the buildout `crew/README.md`, and the rubric's own calibration record cites an external test fixture, `dev/workspace/runs/agent-spec-interviewer/experiments/spec.v1.start.json`, that isn't part of this repo). So the shapes above are reconstructed precisely from the two normative documents, not paraphrased from a sample I can show you.

---

## 2. What the human is actually asked

**What the builder decides for itself (never asked):**

&gt; "Never ask what the intention, the template or a return already states. Never ask the provider to approve a node you wrote." — `spec-builder.md:27`
&gt; "The obvious is written straight into the spec without asking: what the intention, the template and a certain return already state." — `interview-session-conventions.md:31`

A `certain` explorer return with a pointer becomes a spec node immediately, in that same pass — no question asked. Only `probable` or `guess` returns get turned into problems (`spec-builder.md:25, 41`).

**What gets escalated:**

&gt; "The hard is asked: edges, tradeoffs, failure modes and the parts the provider would not think to volunteer." — `interview-session-conventions.md:32`

Given the problem *kinds* named in the register (`gap, dual-path, tradeoff, failure-mode, hard-part`), the concrete shape of a question the provider sees is dictated by which kind it is:

- **gap** — a missing decision nothing in the intention or repo answers: *"The intention says exports go to 'the usual place'. Explorer found two plausible sinks: `S3://reports/` (used by the weekly job) and `exports/` in the repo (used by the CLI's `--out` default). Which is this feature's output location?"* — with options and a recommendation, because a certain-but-ambiguous explorer return is exactly what turns "obvious" into "hard" (`interview-session-conventions.md:33`).
- **dual-path** — two competent readers would build it differently: *"When a duplicate row arrives, should it overwrite the existing record or be rejected as a conflict?"* with both readings stated as options.
- **tradeoff** — a non-negotiable the provider hasn't voiced: *"Constraint C2 could be phrased as a hard latency ceiling or a soft target with alerting. Which — and what's the number?"*
- **failure-mode** — an edge nobody would volunteer: *"What happens when the referenced dependency is offline at the moment of the call — retry, queue, or fail the request?"*
- **hard-part** — the thing that's hard precisely because there's no existing seam: *"Is there an existing retry/backoff utility this should reuse, or is a new one being declared?"*

Each question in a bundle is packaged with **an explanation** (why it matters), **options** (where the answer is a choice), **a recommendation "where you have grounds"** (`spec-builder.md:27`), and **a comment field** so the provider can push back without picking an option outright. The provider is free to ignore the recommendation — "the choice is theirs" (`interview-session-conventions.md:35`).

Note what never appears as a question: approval of prose the builder already wrote, or anything a certain, cited explorer return already settled (`spec-builder.md:27, 41`).

---

## 3. The dispatch chain

The order is fixed and stated as "the heartbeat" (`spec-builder.md:20-31`):

1. **Explorer wave for the intention** — before any question at all, the builder dispatches explorers to map what already exists that the intention touches (`spec-builder.md:24`).
2. **Write the obvious** — first draft, every domain gets at least one node or "empty by decision" (`spec-builder.md:25`).
3. **Register the holes** — problems filed with kinds (`spec-builder.md:26`).
4. **Explorer wave per domain, then ask** — one wave *before* each domain's first question, then bundles go to the provider (`spec-builder.md:27`).
5. **Place the plugs** — answers become nodes or new problems or, if nobody at the table can plug the hole, get sent to an explorer with "a wider net: the web, other repositories" (`spec-builder.md:28`).
6. **Judge** — the instant the register has nothing open/asked, open questions is empty, and the linter exits 0, the spec-judge is dispatched *without waiting for a message from the human* (`spec-builder.md:29`). Every failing rubric question becomes a ledger entry (absorbed or asked), then the judge runs again.
7. **Attack** — a judge verdict of "ready to freeze" (with open questions still empty) dispatches the spec-attacker **in the same turn**, no human approval needed (`spec-builder.md:30`). Findings go through the same ledger; loop returns to step 5 until the attacker returns the literal `no findings`.
8. **Hand over** — closing message with a fenced JSON handoff block. "Freezing is the provider's act; on their instruction, and only then, you perform the mechanics." (`spec-builder.md:31, 73`)

**Who ends each loop:**
- The judge/attacker loop ends when the attacker returns `no findings` *and* open questions is empty (`spec-attacker.md:45`, `spec-builder.md:30`).
- The whole build ends only when the human gives the freeze instruction — no agent in this chain freezes anything (`spec-builder.md:69`, `spec-judge.md` and `spec-attacker.md` both state they never edit or propose).

**Dispatch discipline** — this is what keeps the judge and attacker independent instruments rather than an echo chamber:

&gt; "Dispatch by agent name and pass no model: each dispatched role carries its model in its own definition. Dispatch prompts carry exactly these inputs. No answer text, no bundle, no transcript excerpt reaches a judge or an attacker, because their value is that they read the words the way downstream agents will, without your intent to fill the gaps." — `spec-builder.md:45`

Judge gets exactly two files (rubric + draft, nothing else — `spec-judge.md:12-17`); attacker gets exactly the draft plus read access to the project root, explicitly denied the standard, the rubric, or the interview (`spec-attacker.md:11-12`).

**Explorer's role — how the builder gets project facts without reading source itself.** The builder's own read list is deliberately narrow:

&gt; "You do not open repository files, prior specs, reference pages or history: every fact about the repository or the world reaches you as an explorer return carrying a citation, and a claim without a citation is a guess." — `spec-builder.md:12`

Each explorer dispatch is one question, one stage, optional scope paths; it returns `{ answer, confidence: certain|probable|guess, pointers: [path], candidates: [{domain, text}] }` (`spec-builder.md:41`). Only a `certain` return with a pointer verifies a name and can become a node; anything weaker is written `(unchecked)` in the handoff (`spec-builder.md:41, 73`). This is the mechanism that keeps the builder's own context "narrow… because bulk context dilutes the directives you were given" (`spec-builder.md:11`) — investigation happens in disposable, cited subagent contexts, and only a screenful of cited answer ever returns.

**Important operational gap on `main` right now:** the explorer *is* a real, fully specified agent — I found its complete definition on the `flightcrew-buildout-v1` branch (`flightdeck/flightcrew/crew/explorer.md`: frontmatter `name: explorer, tools: Read, Grep, Glob, Bash, model: haiku, maxTurns: 12`) — but that file, and its dispatch template (`templates/explorer-dispatch.template.md`), are **not present on `main`**. Only `spec-builder.md`, `spec-judge.md`, `spec-attacker.md`, `test-builder.md`, `worker.md`, `reviewer.md` (plus unrelated `adversary.md`, `flight-assistant.md`) are installed under `.claude/agents/` on this branch, confirmed byte-identical to their `flightdeck/flightcrew/crew/*.md` sources. If you dispatch the spec-builder chain exactly as checked out on `main` today, its "Explorer wave" step names an agent (`Agent` tool, dispatch by name) that has no corresponding `.claude/agents/explorer.md` to resolve to — you'd need to either pull `explorer.md` and `explorer-dispatch.template.md` from `flightcrew-buildout-v1`, or have the model build the explorer subagent call inline from the `explorer` role definition already present in `flightdeck/flightcrew/crew/crew.json:293-342` (which fully specifies its purpose, tools, model tier, and `{answer, confidence, pointers}` return contract even without a standalone `.md`).

---

## 4. The exact freeze act

Freezing is explicitly a human decision, never something an agent decides on its own initiative, and it is a **hand instruction that triggers a scripted set of edits**, not a separate `fc freeze` command on `main` (there is no `fc` binary at all on `main` — see caveat above).

**What the human does, physically:** tells the spec-builder to freeze, once every gate is clear:

&gt; "Refuse, listing the blockers, while any open question or unclosed problem exists or the validator fails under `--for-freeze`." — `spec-builder.md:69`

The validator referenced is a real script and command: `flightdeck/flightcrew/checks/validators/validate-spec.mjs`, invoked as:

```
node validate-spec.mjs &lt;spec file&gt; [--schema &lt;file&gt;] --for-freeze
```

`--for-freeze` applies the frozen gates (commit present, open_questions empty) even while `status` still reads `draft` — it answers "could this freeze *now*?" (`validate-spec.mjs:7-10`). Before that, the linter has to have already passed:

```
node spec-readiness-lint.mjs &lt;spec file&gt; --repo &lt;dir&gt; [--run-commands] [--deliverable &lt;path&gt;]...
```
exit 0 required (`spec-readiness-lint.mjs:40`), and the rubric judge (`spec-readiness-rubric.md`) has to have returned "ready to freeze" with a subsequent clean attacker pass.

**What fields change on freeze**, per `spec-builder.md:69` and `flightdeck/manuals/versioning/spec-versioning.md`:

&gt; "On the provider's freeze instruction: set `frozen`, write the commit hash and the reason, and commit the spec file alone." — `spec-builder.md:69`

Concretely, in the header of `spec.v1.json`:
- `status`: `"draft"` → `"frozen"`
- `commit`: absent → the hash the file was frozen at
- `reason`: filled in ("initial, from interview" is the canonical example given in the versioning manual — `spec-versioning.md:38`)

Validator invariant 3/4 enforce this pairing mechanically: a frozen spec without a commit, or a draft carrying a commit, both fail (`validate-spec.mjs:85-94`).

**What commits it:** the spec-builder itself, on instruction — "commit the spec file alone" (`spec-builder.md:69`, echoed in `interview-session-conventions.md:67`: "the interviewer performs the freeze mechanics and commits the spec file alone, refusing while any open question, unclosed problem or validator error remains"). Nothing else — no interview files, no bundle files — is part of that commit; per the buildout branch's `crew/README.md`, the `interview/` subdirectory is never copied into a launch and isn't meant to ride along with the frozen artefact.

**Is there an `fc` freeze command?** Not on `main` as checked out — there is no `bin/fc` at all here (only `bin/worker/render-worker-prompt.mjs` and a `runners.keep` placeholder exist under `flightdeck/flightcrew/bin/`). On the `flightcrew-buildout-v1` branch a full `fc` CLI exists (`bin/fc`, `bin/fc.mjs`, `bin/cmd/*.mjs` including `validate.mjs`, `lint.mjs`, `launch.mjs`) but its command list on that branch has no dedicated `fc spec freeze` subcommand either — freezing is described everywhere, on every branch I found, as the spec-builder's hand-performed mechanics on the provider's instruction, gated by running the validator and linter scripts directly. So: **it is a hand edit performed by the agent under a direct human go-ahead, gated by two real, runnable Node scripts, not a dedicated one-shot freeze command.**

Once frozen, the file becomes permanently read-only in spirit: "A version file is never edited after it is frozen. A revision is a new file with `version` incremented." (`spec-versioning.md:14`). IDs become permanent at this exact moment too — `spec.v1.json`'s IDs may be freely renumbered right up until this first freeze, and never again after (`spec-versioning.md:51`).

---

## 5. Where the tests map comes from after that

**Who:** the test-builder agent, `flightdeck/flightcrew/crew/test-builder.md` (installed identically at `.claude/agents/test-builder.md`, frontmatter `name: test-builder, model: fable`, no `tools:` line in this version — earlier crew.json role spec calls for `Read, Grep, Glob, Bash, Write, Edit`).

**Inputs:** explicitly "the spec, plus whatever the owner points you at: a tests-map template or schema, a reference fixture, the project itself, existing check conventions" (`test-builder.md:11`). It is barred from seeing anything upstream that would let it write a test from the implementation instead of the spec:

&gt; "You must not read any plan, any prior implementation of this spec, or any work-in-progress toward it. If the project already contains an implementation of what the spec describes, derive every check from the spec's words and ids alone — the existing behaviour is exactly what you must not encode." — `test-builder.md:13`

**What it produces:** one named check per behaviour/edge case (named after the ids it covers, "so the mapping is auditable without reading the code" — `test-builder.md:32`), contract checks for every interface, exactly one end-to-end acceptance proof per spec (`test-builder.md:20-21`), and it picks the check *shape* per behaviour from eight named classes — behaviour, contract, acceptance, golden-artefact, structural-validation, visual-comparison, property, rule checks (`test-builder.md:19-26`) — matched to the project's medium, "which is not always a codebase" (frontmatter description).

**Honesty about gaps is structural, not optional:**

&gt; "Anything you could not cover goes in the map as explicitly unverified, with the reason — never silently." — `test-builder.md:34`
&gt; "If a spec statement is too vague to write a check from without interpreting it, do not guess… Stop on that item, record it as an open question against the spec id, and report it." — `test-builder.md:36`

**The baseline** — this is the specific step you asked about, and it is a real behavioural claim, not a formality:

&gt; "When the checks exist, run everything once against the project as it stands, and record the results. The expected picture: rule and structural checks that concern existing material pass; every check for behaviour that does not yet exist fails, and fails for the right reason — the absence of the thing, not a broken harness, a missing fixture, or an error in the check itself. Fix any check that fails wrongly. The baseline is the proof that the targets are real: a run that later turns these red results green has demonstrably built what the spec asked for." — `test-builder.md:38-40`

In other words: the baseline run is a deliberate **red run**, executed once, on the *pre-implementation* codebase, whose purpose is to prove each new check fails because the feature is genuinely absent — not because the check itself is broken. A check that passes before the feature exists is treated as a defective check and must be fixed before hand-off. The handoff then states where the checks and map live, the coverage summary, the single command that runs everything, and the baseline results, with an explicit warning that from this point the checks are locked targets and any conflict between a check and the spec is a human decision (`test-builder.md:42-44`).

---

## 6. Does `design.md` fit in this process, or is it a one-off?

It's a one-off, and a very specific kind of one-off: **`flightdeck/launch/specs/flightcrew-v1/design.md` is not a pre-spec artefact this process produces for ordinary features — it is a build contract for building the flightcrew orchestration system itself**, written for the self-hosted project where FlightDeck's own agents built FlightDeck.

I traced it in git history rather than the working tree, because it doesn't exist on `main`: it was added at commit `5f69a94 Add flightcrew-v1 spec, design contract, and Claude Code facts sheet`, on the ancestry that became `flightcrew-buildout-v1`, and it never merges into `main`. Its own opening lines say exactly what it is:

&gt; "This document is the contract every build agent works from. It fixes the directory map, file ownership, CLI surface, file formats, hook behaviour, crew roster, and the run journey. The system spec `spec.v1.json` beside it states the behaviours the testbench proves; this document states the contracts those behaviours rely on. Where this document and an agent's judgement disagree, this document wins; where this document and the spec disagree, the spec wins…" — `design.md` (via `git show 5f69a94:flightdeck/launch/specs/flightcrew-v1/design.md`, lines 1-3)

So it sits *beside* a spec, not *instead of* one, and it's explicitly subordinate to it ("where this document and the spec disagree, the spec wins"). It exists because this particular project was unusual: the thing being specified was the spec-building machinery, agent roster, hooks, and CLI itself, so someone needed to fix ground rules — sandbox boundaries, runtime constraints ("Node 22 ES modules… no `package.json`, no `jq`. macOS."), byte-unchanged files, unit ownership — that a normal nine-domain spec doesn't carry (the spec conventions manual is explicit that "conduct" — tools, agents, gates, displays — never belongs in a spec: `spec-conventions.md:33, 75`). `design.md` is where that conduct-level material went instead.

Nothing in `spec-builder.md`, `spec-judge.md`, `spec-attacker.md`, `test-builder.md`, the spec templates/schema, or the interview conventions references a `design.md` as a required or even optional input to an ordinary interview. The builder's required session inputs are just: the intention (or a draft's Intent node), the template, the schema, the project root, and the run folder (`spec-builder.md:18`). A `design.md` is therefore not a step in "idea to frozen spec" for a normal feature — it's a bootstrapping artefact this repository produced exactly once, for the meta-project of building the chain you're now asking about, and it doesn't recur elsewhere in the repository's history (no other `design.md` exists anywhere in the git history of this repo).</result>

[ASSISTANT 💬]
The spec agent came back, and question 2 has a proper answer — plus one thing you'll want to know before you try to run the chain today.

## There *is* an interview, and it's a file protocol, not a chat script

Three files under a run folder, and they are the whole mechanism:

- **`interview/problems.json`** — the register. Every hole the builder can name gets filed with a kind: `gap`, `dual-path`, `tradeoff`, `failure-mode`, `hard-part`, "or whatever the hole actually is." Each cites the explorer return or the sentence of the intention that revealed it. Lifecycle: registered → asked → closed, or reopened if the answer revealed a new hole.
- **`interview/bundles/<marker>.json`** — one bundle per problem, **at most five questions**, each with an explanation, options where the answer is a choice, a recommendation *where the builder has grounds*, and a comment field. The bundle itself takes a comment.
- **`interview/findings.json`** — the ledger for judge failures and attacker findings, each resolved to exactly one of `asked`, `absorbed`, `rejected with a reason`.

**On your "so no kickoff prompt?" instinct — for the spec, the answer is the opposite, and deliberately so:**

> "How a bundle is shown to the provider — a page, the question tool, plain chat — is a surface's concern and never yours; the bundle file is the contract."

That's an explicit seam. `AskUserQuestion` is one surface; a rendered page is another; handing you the JSON to edit is a third. Answers come back in a defined chat block — `## <marker>`, then `### <id>`, `answer:`, optional `comment:`, then an optional `bundle comment:` — and several bundles may return in one block.

**What you're never asked:** anything the intention, the template, or a `certain` explorer return already states — and never to approve a node the builder wrote. Only `probable` or `guess` returns become problems. That's the whole economy of the thing: the builder writes the obvious itself and spends your attention on edges, tradeoffs, failure modes, and "the parts the provider would not think to volunteer."

## The freeze is a hand act, and there is no command for it

I checked both branches: **no `fc spec freeze` exists anywhere.** You tell the builder to freeze; it refuses while any open question or unclosed problem exists or `validate-spec --for-freeze` fails. On your go-ahead it sets `status: frozen`, writes the commit hash and the reason, and **commits the spec file alone** — the interview files never ride along. From that moment IDs are permanent and the file is never edited again; a revision is a new numbered file.

## The thing you should know before running this on `main`

Two pieces of the chain are missing on the branch you're on:

- **`spec-interviewer.md` is zero bytes** — you already know, STRUCTURE.md says it's what the next launch builds.
- **`explorer.md` is not installed either.** The spec-builder's own heartbeat opens with an explorer wave and dispatches by agent name, but there's no `explorer` in `.claude/agents/` on `main`. The role *is* fully specified in `crew/crew.json` (purpose, tools, model tier, the `{answer, confidence, pointers}` return contract), so the definition exists — it just isn't installed as a dispatchable agent.

So if you dispatch `spec-builder` today, step one of its method names an agent that won't resolve.

One correction to something I said earlier: `design.md` is not part of this process. It's a one-off bootstrapping contract written for building flightcrew itself, sits *beside* a spec rather than before one, and explicitly loses to the spec where they disagree. No other `design.md` exists in the repo's history.

Dug it up. You're right on all counts, and the evidence is unambiguous.

## The launch folder comes first. I had it backwards.

Two run folders in history have exactly the shape you're describing:

```
flightdeck/launch/reference-library/          (constitution-research)
├── idea.txt                    ← the seed, free prose
├── interview/
│   ├── problems.json           ← the register
│   ├── findings.json           ← the judge/attacker ledger
│   └── bundles/P1…P5.json      ← the questions put to you
└── spec.v1.json                ← the output

flightdeck/launch/agent-spec-interviewer/     (engage-crew)
├── interview/{problems,findings}.json + bundles/
├── judge/verdict-v1.md, verdict-v1-run3…run8.md
├── attacker/findings-v1-run1.md
└── spec.v1.json
```

The spec is **grown inside the run folder**, alongside the interview that produced it and the judge and attacker verdicts that tested it. A spec is a run artifact. Your read is the one the actual practice supports.

What I documented is `fc launch new <spec-path>` on the buildout branch, which takes a path to an *already frozen* spec and errors `no spec at <given>` if it isn't there. That's the busted bit you're pointing at: the CLI hardcodes spec-first, which is the opposite of how every real spec in this repo was actually made.

And there's no single agreed shape — there are **three**, one per branch: `launch/specs/flightcrew-v1/` (buildout), `launch/agent-types/specs/` (engage-crew), and `launch/<run>/spec.v1.json` (constitution-research, engage-crew). Your theory about the external folder holds up: it's the only one of its kind, and the buildout branch's `launch/README.md` declared it "the canonical, cross-run home" *after the fact* — documenting what the orchestrator had already improvised.

One more: **no launch-local `checks/` has ever existed, on any branch, in any commit.** STRUCTURE.md's `example-launch/checks/` was never built. Nor was `example-launch` itself — no folder by that name appears anywhere in history; it's purely illustrative.

## Kickoff: 7 files, but nothing makes it 7

"So far" is precisely right. Seven files sit in the directory; **nothing in the code enumerates them.** `loadParts` does `path.join(kickoffDir, name + '.md')` and checks the file exists. Drop in `task-refactor.md` with `<!-- version: 1 -->` as its first line, pass `--kickoff base+shape-session+task-refactor`, and it renders — no registry, no enum, no schema. The only rules: `base` first, at least one `shape-`, at least one `task-`.

On `main` there are **zero** kickoff parts — the directory doesn't exist. `liftoff.template.yaml` replaced the idea entirely.

## How a spec appears — with a real one

`idea.txt` for the reference-library run is about eight lines of prose: what to build, the lens it's viewed through, who it's for, what goes wrong without it, what success looks like, then `phase 1:`. That's the entire seed.

From there the builder runs an explorer wave, then files what it can't resolve into `problems.json` — each with `id`, `kind`, `domain`, `status`, a `statement`, and the `evidence` that revealed it. A real one:

> **P2** · `tradeoff` · constraints · *asked* — "Placement and shipping: `library/` at repo root is the stated home for human-facing persistent documents but does not travel with an installed deck, while the intention names agents among the readers. Where do entries, register and index live, and does the deck carry them?"

Its bundle is what actually reaches you — one question with an explanation of why it matters, three options, and a recommendation with grounds:

> *"library/<subfolder>/ at repo root — asset authoring happens here, and STRUCTURE.md already defines library/ as exactly this kind of document."*

Answers close problems, and the register records *how*: P1's `closed_by` reads *"Provider supplied the topic list at …orchestration-concepts-tools.md"* — so answering can mean handing over a document, not just picking an option. Then judge, then attacker, looping — `agent-spec-interviewer` has eight judge verdicts on one spec version.

So: **idea.txt → explorers → problems → bundles → your answers → judge → attacker → freeze**, all inside the run folder. The run folder is created by you, by hand, with an idea in it. That's what starts it.

Both published pages have this wrong. Want me to correct them, or is this still moving too much to be worth pinning down?

[ASSISTANT 💬]
Let me take the checks question seriously, because I think you've found a real category error.

## There are three different things called "checks"

They have three different lifetimes, and the buildout branch collapsed them into one:

**The project's test code.** Rails' `test/`, a Python project's `tests/`. Permanent, belongs to the project, outlives every run and exists whether or not flightcrew ever touches the repo.

**The targets for one spec version** — T7 covers B12, expects fail at baseline, runs *this* command. Born with the tests-map, dies when that spec version is superseded. Per-run.

**The results** — exit code, output tail, at commit X, attempt N. Pure evidence. Per-run, per-attempt.

## You're right about the 29, and the tell is in the data

Look at what a check actually is on the buildout branch. `T1`'s command, from its own evidence file:

```
node flightdeck/testbench/suites/e2e/run.mjs
```

That's a *pointer*. The check is one line of shell; the suite is the test. Which means the wrapper layer you're describing already exists implicitly — it just never got named, so the thing it points at had to be built somewhere flightcrew owned.

And the reason it landed in `flightdeck/testbench/` is self-hosting: the project being built *was* flightdeck, so "the project's tests" and "flightcrew's assets" had the same address and nobody had to decide which was which. In any other repo the 29 would obviously be `test/`, and `testbench/` would hold nothing but the wrapper and the runner. The `tests.keep` file even hints at the confusion — "reusable across the git branch, save to main to access globally, merge protect this folder" is describing a project's test suite, not run infrastructure.

So: **`testbench/` as a home for suites is an artifact of flightcrew building itself.** It doesn't generalise, and shipping it as scaffold would put a `testbench/` in every Rails app that already has `test/`.

## Where things land, if you split by lifetime

- **Universal** → `flightcrew/`: the wrapper, the runner, schemas, validators, crew, templates. Things every run needs and no run owns.
- **Project-permanent** → wherever the language puts tests. flightcrew doesn't own it, doesn't create it, and writes a *wrapper* to invoke it.
- **Per-run** → the run folder: the tests-map (which behaviour maps to which check, with baselines), the check scripts that have no natural project home, and the results.

The middle one is the piece that makes it portable. A check becomes `id + covers + baseline + how to invoke`, and "how to invoke" is `bin/rails test test/models/export_test.rb` — the project's own idiom, not a bespoke `run.mjs`.

**And you already built this.** It's in `liftoff.template.yaml`:

> `check_wrapper: flightdeck/bin/check` — runs a check by id, records output under `directories.checks`, exits with the check's code

with `check_commands:` carrying empty slots for `unit`, `contract`, `artefact`, `acceptance`, `lint`, `types`, `build`. That's exactly the wrapping layer. The buildout branch has no wrapper — each check carries its command inline — which is precisely why every check had to become a hand-written script in a folder flightcrew controlled.

## So yes, a `checks/` in the run — but you have a name collision

Two different things want that name:

- **liftoff.yaml and STRUCTURE.md** mean *results*: "one file per check run, named by check id and attempt."
- **The buildout branch's `launch/specs/<name>/checks/`** means *scripts with no natural project home* — the escape hatch for a check that can't live in `test/` because it's checking documentation shape or a structural rule.

Both are legitimately per-run. I'd separate them: `<run>/checks/` for the scripts this spec needed and the project couldn't host, `<run>/evidence/` for results — evidence is already the better word for a result, and the buildout branch had that right. Attempt-numbering then lives inside evidence, where it belongs.

## On shapes versus the 80-node YAML

Your instinct is worth trusting, and the reason is structural: **a kickoff part is a coherent doctrine authored as a whole; eighty independent switches are eighty chances to produce an incoherent run.** `shape-workflow` was written by someone holding the whole picture. A YAML where you uncomment `isolation: none` and `unit_adversary: off` and `baseline.required: false` individually has no author checking the combination makes sense.

The failure mode is already visible in both designs. The kickoff renderer uses `.find()`, so `base+shape-session+shape-workflow` renders happily with two contradictory dispatch doctrines glued together. The YAML has the same hazard eighty times over, with the header rule — "never leave two values active for the same key" — enforced by nothing but your discipline.

`missions/shape-library.md` is the reconciliation you already sketched: *"build a shape, record it forever, reuse when ever"* over `+ spec: + liftoff: + plan:`. Make a shape a **named bundle that sets liftoff values**, and the YAML becomes the *resolved output* — the record of what this run was configured to do — rather than the thing you author by hand. You'd still be able to override a key, but you'd start from a coherent position instead of eighty defaults.

## The missing front door — you've named the real gap

There is no "start here." Concretely, what's absent is the inverse of `fc launch new`: something that creates and names the run folder, drops an `idea.txt` stub and a blank `spec.v1.json` from the template with `status: draft`, and tells you the next move is to write the idea and dispatch the builder. Today the folder gets made by hand, and the only reason both real interview runs have the right shape is that whoever ran them already knew it.

That's also exactly what STRUCTURE.md says is next: `spec-interviewer.md` is deliberately empty, "what the next launch builds." The human entry point and the empty file are the same gap.

Want me to work any of these up properly — a folder-layout proposal you could put in STRUCTURE.md, or a spec-shaped writeup of the bootstrap command and the wrapper?

[ASSISTANT 💬]
Definitive answer, and your hypothesis is right — with a bonus finding you'll want.

## test-builder does have a convention, and it's a precedence list

From `crew/test-builder.md`, step 2:

> "Write each check as a suite in **the first of these that applies**: the directory your dispatch names, else the existing test directory the project's checks already live in, else `flightdeck/launch/specs/<spec-name>/checks/`."

So it's already Rails-aware at step two — *"the existing test directory the project's checks already live in."* Drop flightcrew into a Rails app and test-builder will write into `test/` on its own.

**But `flightdeck/testbench/` is nowhere in that list.** test-builder has never heard of it.

## The buildout got there through step one — the dispatch named it

`design.md` is the dispatch, and it mandates testbench explicitly:

- line 21: *"`testbench/` — 'Test harnesses. reusable across the git branch.' The suite runner, one regression suite per spec T id, fixtures, and the rubric bench."*
- line 265: the targets unit's writable paths are `testbench/run-all.mjs`, `testbench/fixtures/**`, `testbench/suites/<name>/**`
- line 275: *"Implementation units may create suites only under `testbench/suites/unit-*/`."*

So exactly as you guessed: the agent didn't derive the testbench convention from the test-building process — **the run's own build contract hardcoded it**, and test-builder obediently took step one of its precedence list. Self-hosting again: the dispatch author and the project author were the same person, so "where my project keeps tests" got written as a run instruction instead of a project convention.

## The bonus finding — the run-local `checks/` existed and was deliberately destroyed

`design.md` line 279, in the instructions for producing the launch folder:

> "(the existing empty `launch/flightcrew-buildout/checks/` and `specs/` folders are removed first; a `checks/README.md` is not needed)"

`launch/flightcrew-buildout/checks/` **was there.** The STRUCTURE.md shape was real, scaffolded, and then explicitly deleted as a build step — which is why my history search found no trace of it. It never got committed, because the contract said remove it first.

So the run-local `checks/` isn't aspirational and it isn't busted-by-accident. It was overridden by a one-off contract written for a self-hosted build, and the override outlived the run.

## Your intention *is* written down — just nowhere an agent looks

`tests.keep` says exactly what you said:

> "Test harnesses. reusable across the git branch. save to `main` to access globally. merge protect this folder. do not store launch test results here. they belong to each launch. `runs/` is the result of local test runs."

That's the convention — testbench as the known home when the project has no natural one, results stay with the launch. It's correct and it's recorded. The problem is that STRUCTURE.md's whole keep-file premise — *"Read them first when working in an unfamiliar folder: several carry rules that are not inferable from the tree"* — depends on someone routing agents to read them, and test-builder's precedence list doesn't.

## The one-line fix

Change test-builder's third fallback. Right now it reads:

> …else `flightdeck/launch/specs/<spec-name>/checks/`

which hardcodes the external-specs shape you've just rejected. It should be `flightdeck/testbench/suites/` — that makes the precedence read as: *the dispatch's choice, else the project's own test directory, else flightdeck's known home for a project that hasn't got one.* Which is precisely the intention in `tests.keep`, and it removes the last thing pushing checks into an external spec folder.

That leaves `<run>/checks/` free to mean what liftoff.yaml and STRUCTURE.md already say it means — results per attempt — and the three layers finally line up: universal in `flightcrew/`, project-permanent in `test/` or `testbench/`, per-run in the run.

[ASSISTANT 💬]
This unravels it, and the answer is already written down — in a file that also shows my last suggestion was wrong.

## The system's distinction isn't yours, but it's compatible

`library/terms.md`: **"check — any executable verification, test or otherwise, that returns pass or fail."**

So a check isn't an overlay on a test. It's the **genus**; a test is one species. `testing-description.md` names seven classes — structural, behavioural, artefact, invariant, project-rule, statistical, judged — and unit tests, contract tests and property tests appear only as *members* of behavioural and invariant. The word "test" never names an artifact in these docs. The tests-map schema's `kind` field carries exactly those seven values, so the classification is already in the code.

Your `skill.md` point isn't just accommodated, it's the reason two of the classes exist. **Three verdicts, not one:**

- **exit code** — deterministic, from structural/behavioural/artefact/invariant/project-rule
- **ratio** — successes over trials against a threshold declared *before* any trial ran, from statistical alone
- **verdict sheet** — fixed questions, scored answers, mandatory quotations, schema-validated first, from judged alone

A `skill.md` gets checked by running **probes** (a fresh agent whose only job is to be an ordinary user) against **scenarios**, N times, producing **records**; the ratio gates, and a **judge** grades the records against a **rubric** into a verdict sheet. With guardrails: every scenario set needs a control where the behaviour must *not* fire, a judged verdict never gates alone, and one transcript chosen at the gate — not by the builder — is read by a human.

So "checks can be things tests aren't" is the design, formalised.

## Where they live — the rule exists, and it says run folder

`testing-conventions.md`, first line of the Schema section:

> **"A check belongs to a spec, and its files live in a directory named for that spec."**

Plus: *"Fixtures and golden outputs are committed beside the checks that use them"* and *"the checks freeze with the spec."*

Checks are **spec-scoped artifacts that freeze with the spec**. Since a spec is a run artifact, checks are run artifacts. You were right, and it was written down the whole time.

**Which means my fix last turn was wrong.** I suggested changing test-builder's third fallback from `launch/specs/<spec-name>/checks/` to `testbench/suites/`. That fallback is *literally* the convention — "a directory named for that spec." Changing it would have broken the stated rule. Ignore that suggestion.

## "Target" is a status, not an artifact

terms.md: **"target — a check that exists before the work and is locked against it."**

Same object as a check, wearing two properties: pre-existing, and locked. Hence *"Humans freeze the targets; no run starts against an unfrozen check set"* and *"A golden output is generated once, inspected by a human, then committed as a target."* The `targets` phase is where checks acquire that status; liftoff's `verification.targets: prebuilt | build-in-run | none` just says whether they have it yet.

So it's not a fourth thing in the verification domain. Check is the noun; target is the check after freezing.

## The real gap — and it's exactly the one you're pointing at

These conventions were written as though **every check is authored for the spec, by the test-writer, from scratch**. Read them against a Rails app and they have nothing to say about `test/`:

- *"The test-writer works from the frozen spec and nothing else"*
- *"Write the checks before the work exists"*
- *"Check files, fixtures and goldens are read-only to implementing agents"*

A Rails suite pre-exists the spec, isn't spec-scoped, is owned by the project, and — your point — doesn't emit anything a gate can read. It prints its own format, has no `covers`, no baseline, no spec IDs.

**That's precisely where your overlay belongs**, and the conventions almost reach it: *"A check returns something the agent reads inside the loop: an exit code, a summary line, a diff"* and *"Traceable: every verdict cites the spec IDs it speaks for."* A native Rails test satisfies neither. So the spec-scoped check that invokes it — supplying `covers`, the baseline, the ID trace, and the gate-readable verdict — is a real object with real work to do, not a redundant wrapper. It's the adapter between a project's native test and an orchestration run.

Which lands your three-layer split, with one addition the docs already provide:

- **Native tests** — project-owned, pre-existing, live where the language puts them. flightcrew never writes or locks these.
- **Checks** — spec-scoped, live in the run folder, freeze with the spec, locked during it. Some wrap a native test; some *are* the whole verification (statistical, judged) because no native test could exist.
- **Graduated checks** — the conventions already describe this: *"a check every spec needs graduates to a hook or a constitution-level command, and leaves individual specs."* Project-rule checks especially, being *"the project's memory made executable."* That's the project-permanent layer, and `testbench/` is a reasonable home for it in a project with nowhere else.

Two inconsistencies worth fixing while you're in here: terms.md and the conventions preamble both say a check *"returns pass or fail"*, which contradicts the three-verdict model — a ratio isn't pass/fail. And `testing-description.md` announces that *"the placements answer where a check lives"*, then defines placement as *who invokes it* (standalone/embedded/chained/enforced), never as a directory. The filesystem answer only exists in the conventions' first line, which is easy to miss.

Want me to draft this as a proper revision — the check/test/target distinction and the three placements — in a form you could drop into the testing manuals?

------------------------------------------------------------

[ASSISTANT 💬]
All fifteen anchors verified verbatim against source.

**`dev/workspace/filebox/testing-manuals-revision.md`** — nine changes across three files, each as an anchor plus replacement block, nothing applied.

**What it fixes**

Four defects, in the order they'd bite someone reading the manuals cold:

- **`check` is defined as returning pass or fail** in both `terms.md` and the conventions preamble — contradicting the three-verdict model those same documents run on. A ratio isn't pass or fail.
- **Nothing names a project's own tests.** The manuals assume every check is authored from the frozen spec by the test-writer, so a Rails suite has no place in the model and the check that adapts one has no name.
- **The filesystem rule is stated once, in passing.** `testing-description.md` promises that "the placements answer *where a check lives*" then defines placement as *what invokes* a check. The directory rule exists only as the first line of the conventions' Schema section.
- **`target` reads as a fourth artefact** beside check, class and verdict, when it's a status a check holds.

**The substantive additions**

`terms.md` gains a `test` entry — *"a project's own pass/fail assertion, owned by the project, predating any spec and outliving every run; a check may invoke one, and many checks have none to invoke"* — and `target` is restated as a status rather than an object.

A new **three homes** section for `testing-description.md`: the project's tests (never written or frozen by a run), the spec's checks (in the run, frozen with the spec), and the project's permanent checks (where the promotion rule already sends things). It states plainly that statistical and judged checks are *always* wholly the spec's, since an agent-shaped artefact has no assertion a project suite could hold — which is your `skill.md` case, given a home.

Two rules I'd draw your eye to, because they're new constraints rather than clarifications:

- **"A project test a check invokes is locked for the run's duration by the check that names it."** This closes a hole the current conventions leave open — the check is locked, but if its assertion lives in a project test the implementer may freely edit, the lock is defeated at one remove. Locking follows invocation.
- **"Where an entry cannot hold a pass/fail assertion at all, verify it by ratio or verdict sheet rather than recording it unverified."** Today the conventions only offer "report the ID as untestable," which is wrong for agent-shaped work — the statistical and judged classes exist so such an entry *is* checkable.

**Three consequences I flagged but didn't fold in**, since each is your call:

`test-builder.md`'s precedence still routes checks into the project's existing test directory, which under the three homes makes a check project-permanent before it's earned promotion. `tests.keep` holds the harness convention correctly and no crew definition points an agent at it. And the tests-map schema carries **two** overlapping vocabularies — `kind` with the seven class names, `class` with `deterministic|property|statistical|judged` — which I confirmed in the schema. Worth settling before another map freezes against both.

------------------------------------------------------------

[ASSISTANT 💬]
The data backs you exactly. One piece of work, two folders, three locations.

## What the launch files actually say

Both runs pin the **identical spec**: `flightcrew-v1`, version 1, commit `5f69a94`, file_commit `88290d0`. Not a revised spec — the same frozen bytes.

The only thing that differs is the tests map: **v1 @ `44aff6b` → v2 @ `5cad9b9`**. Run 2 carries `previous_launch: flightcrew-buildout`. Run 1 ended 22:43 on the 3rd; run 2 ended 02:47 on the 4th. Four hours apart, same night, same target.

That is one run, attempted twice — and the system had no way to say so, so it said it three times in three different shapes:

- **`flightcrew-buildout/`** — attempt one's evidence, returns, report
- **`flightcrew-buildout-2/`** — attempt two's, with a backpointer instead of a counter
- **`launch/specs/flightcrew-v1/`** — the spec and *both* map versions, hoisted out of either run because neither could hold a version series

## The mechanical cause

`fc launch new` refuses a directory that already exists — `launch exists: <path>`. There is no attempt concept anywhere in `launch.json`; the only version-ish key in either file is `schema_version`. So a second pass at the same spec **structurally cannot** reuse the directory. The CLI forces a sibling.

`previous_launch` is the tell. It's computed automatically — the newest other launch of the same spec. The system detected the relationship and recorded it, while having no way to express it as one thing. That's a workaround standing in for a missing field.

Run 2 also quietly abandoned the branch convention: `run/flightcrew-buildout` became `flightcrew-buildout-v1`. Once the directory identity broke, the branch identity followed.

## liftoff already has the fix

Your newer design models this correctly and explicitly:

> `attempt: 1` — *"increments per attempt at the same spec version; names the version branch"*
> `version_branch: ${git.run_branch}/${references.spec_version}-a${run.attempt}`

and the discriminator that makes one directory work for many attempts:

> `checks: ${directories.run_root}/checks/` — *"one file per check run, named by check id **and attempt**"*

So: **one directory per run, attempts distinguished by branch and by filename, spec and map versions living in place.** Which is what you just described. The PR section carries it through too — one PR per attempt, and *"the next attempt is a new version branch and a new PR, and the old one stays as the record."*

## One refinement to your phrasing

You said "a v2+ spec, and v1 failing." Here the **spec held at v1** — it was the *tests map* that went to v2, because the defect was in two checks, not in the spec. Worth keeping distinct, because a second attempt can bump the spec, the map, or neither:

- **neither** — a pure retry after a transient failure or a budget ceiling
- **map only** — what actually happened: a check was wrong
- **spec** — a real spec-conflict finding, which is the one case that genuinely ends the run rather than the attempt

All three belong in the same run directory. Only the third changes what "done" means.

## The downstream symptom

`RUNLOG.md` now has two entries for one piece of work — and the planner reads RUNLOG before cutting a plan for the same spec. So the next run sees two prior runs rather than one run with two attempts, and the second entry's reservation (*"the tests map was revised to v2 mid-build rather than after the human's log"*) reads as a fresh observation instead of the consequence of the first entry's failure. The history loses its causality at exactly the point it's meant to teach.

Want me to draft this as a second revision in the filebox — the run/attempt/version model, what `launch.json` needs (an `attempt` field, spec and map versions as series rather than pins), and what `fc launch new` would have to stop refusing?

------------------------------------------------------------

[ASSISTANT 💬]
Two diagnosis documents in `dev/workspace/filebox/`, alongside the revision draft you edited.

**`testing-problems.md`** — ten findings. Five you'd already put your finger on; five the digging turned up. The one I'd flag as new and worth your attention is **the schema carrying two overlapping vocabularies**: every check has a required `kind` with the seven class names, and an optional `class` with `deterministic, property, statistical, judged`. Neither field has a description. They overlap on `statistical` and `judged`; `property` is a technique inside the invariant class, not a class; and `deterministic` isn't a class at all — it's one of the three *verdicts*. `terms.md` compounds it by defining both `class` and `check class` as separate near-identical entries. Two maps have already frozen against both fields.

Also in there: the lock is defeasible at one remove (a check whose assertion lives in a file it doesn't contain is locked while the deciding file isn't — currently true by accident, since nothing invokes outside its own suite), and the conventions and the description document give **opposite instructions** for an entry with no pass/fail assertion.

**`run-problems.md`** — thirteen findings. The spine of it is that the schema has no `attempt` property, `launch.json` pins exactly one spec and one map for life, and `launch.mjs:267` refuses an existing directory unconditionally. Given a failed first attempt, the CLI admits no shape other than a sibling folder. The version series then had nowhere to live inside a run, which is why it was hoisted to `launch/specs/` — and that hoisted shape was later written up in `launch/README.md` as "the canonical, cross-run home", then propagated into `test-builder.md` as its final fallback.

The finding I'd read first is **10**: the run log holds two entries for one piece of work, the second entry's reservation is the direct consequence of the first's abandonment, and the planner reads that file before planning against the same spec. The causal link survives only in a `previous_launch` field the run log doesn't carry — so the record kept in order to learn from failure is the one place the failure's shape is lost.

Two things I noticed while writing and left as findings rather than smoothing over: `launch.mjs` line 300 contains a comment showing the author already knew about the `launch exists` retry problem, and the three incompatible run-folder shapes across branches resolve to a single irony — **the layout the tooling enforces is the one no spec was ever made under, and the layout every spec was actually made under is the one no tooling supports.**
